[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n15\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF45.2, F42\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I guess… I came in because I keep getting really stuck on this tiny uneven spot around my nose and cheek. It’s not even like I can explain it well, but in photos, or when I’m at work under those bright store lights, it just jumps out at me. Like that’s all I can see.\n\nOther people say they don’t notice it, or they’ll say I look fine, but it doesn’t really feel that way to me. It feels like it’s the first thing customers see when they come up to the register, and like my managers must notice it too, even if they don’t say anything. I know it probably sounds small, but it’s been on my mind a lot, and I’m just tired of feeling so uncomfortable about it.\n\nDoctor: Has this concern about your appearance been affecting your work or relationships with others?\n\nPatient: Yeah… mostly at work, I think. I mean, I try to act normal, and I don’t think I’m like completely falling apart or anything, but my manager has noticed I’m coming back from my breaks late. I’ll go to the bathroom or somewhere with a mirror and then I end up taking longer than I meant to, and when I come back I can tell she’s kind of watching the time.\n\nAnd at the register I’ve been moving slower too, like I get distracted and I’m not as quick with customers as I usually am. My manager hasn’t yelled at me or anything, but she has made comments, like reminding me to keep things moving or asking if everything’s okay. And that makes me really nervous because I’m trying to get promoted, and I don’t want them thinking I’m unreliable or not focused enough. That’s probably the biggest thing—feeling like this stupid concern is making me look bad right when I’m trying to prove I can handle more.\n\nDoctor: Is it just this one spot on your face that bothers you, or are there other parts of your appearance that you worry about?\n\nPatient: It’s mostly that one spot, honestly. Like, the area around my nose and cheek where it looks uneven to me. I don’t know if “spot” is even the right word, because sometimes it’s more like the shape or the texture or how the light hits it, especially in photos or under the lights at work.\n\nI guess other things can bother me a little, like anyone, but this is the thing I get stuck on. Even when people tell me they don’t see anything, I still feel like it’s obvious, like customers at the register notice it right away or my managers are looking at it when they talk to me. It’s hard to explain because I know it sounds tiny, but it feels really noticeable to me.\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding social situations or events because of your concern about your appearance?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure how to answer that. I don’t think I’m, like, fully avoiding everything or canceling plans all the time because of it. It’s more that I’m aware of it when I’m around people, if that makes sense.\n\nLike if someone wants to take a picture, I’ll get uncomfortable, or I’ll start thinking about the lighting or which side of my face is showing, and then I’m not really enjoying myself as much. But I don’t know that I’ve stopped going to events completely. I guess I haven’t really thought of it as “avoiding social situations,” exactly. It’s more just… it’s there in the back of my mind when I’m with people.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself repeating certain actions over and over, like checking your appearance in the mirror, in response to your concerns about the spot on your face?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. Probably more than I really want to admit. It’s not like I plan to do it, but on my breaks at work I’ll go into the bathroom and check my face in the mirror, especially that area by my nose and cheek. I’ll look at it from different angles, kind of tilt my head or move closer and farther away, and then I’ll start messing with my hair to try to cover it or make it less noticeable.\n\nAnd then it turns into this whole thing where I’m like, “Okay, just one more second,” but I keep checking again because the lighting looks different or I’m not sure if it looks worse than it did before. Sometimes I’ll compare myself to girls who come through my line too, like noticing their skin or face shape and thinking they look normal and I don’t. I hate that I do that, because they’re just customers and I’m supposed to be working, but my brain goes there.\n\nIt can make my breaks run long because I get stuck in the mirror, and then I’m late getting back to the register. Not by, like, an hour or anything, but enough that it’s been noticed. So yeah, I guess it is repetitive. It feels embarrassing to say, but I do keep checking and fixing and comparing.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like checking your appearance in the mirror helps you feel better or less anxious about the spot on your face?\n\nPatient: For like… a minute, maybe. It’s weird, because I go in there thinking if I just look at it and make sure it’s not as bad as I’m imagining, then I’ll feel calmer and go back to work. And sometimes I do feel a little better if I fix my hair around it or if the mirror lighting makes it look not as noticeable.\n\nBut then it kind of flips. I’ll check from one angle, then another, and then I’m not sure which one is the “real” way people are seeing me. So I keep looking, and if it looks bad in that bathroom mirror, I get more anxious. And even if it looks okay, I don’t totally trust it, because then I think, well, what if it looks different out by the register, or under the store lights, or when customers are standing close?\n\nSo I guess it helps for a second, but not really in a lasting way. I end up fixing my hair around that side of my face and comparing myself to girls who come through my line, and then I’m thinking about it even more. Sometimes I take so long doing all of that on my break that I’m late getting back to the register, which makes me feel embarrassed too.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to check your appearance in the mirror, or is it something you can choose not to do if you want to?\n\nPatient: It feels like I should be able to choose not to, because technically no one is forcing me to go look in the bathroom mirror. But in the moment it doesn’t feel that simple. Like on my break, I’ll tell myself, “Just don’t check, just get your drink or use the bathroom and go back,” but then I start feeling really aware of that spot on my face, and I’ll think maybe my hair moved or maybe it looks worse under the store lights, and then it’s like I can’t really relax until I check.\n\nSo I end up going to the mirror and looking at it, and then I fix my hair around that side of my face, and then I’ll check again from a slightly different angle. It’s embarrassing because it’s not like one quick glance and I’m done. I can get stuck there longer than I meant to, and then I’m late getting back to the register sometimes.\n\nAnd even when I try not to look, I still find myself comparing my face to girls who come through my line, like noticing their skin or their features and thinking they look fine and I don’t. So… I guess I can choose not to in theory, but it feels really hard not to once the thought is there.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're able to stop checking your appearance in the mirror if you want to?\n\nPatient: Not really… I mean, I can sometimes force myself to walk away, but it doesn’t feel easy or like I’m just casually choosing. On breaks at work especially, I’ll tell myself I’m not going to check, because I know if I go into the bathroom mirror it can turn into a whole thing. But then I start wondering if that spot on my face is showing more, or if my hair isn’t covering it right, and I feel like I need to look.\n\nAnd once I do look, it’s hard to stop at one time. I’ll check my face, then fix my hair around that area, then check again to see if it looks better. Sometimes I’ll move around a little because the angle or lighting makes it look different, and then I’m standing there longer than I meant to. I’ll think, “Okay, I’m done,” but then I look one more time before leaving.\n\nIt’s embarrassing because it’s not like I want to spend my break doing that. I’m supposed to just go back to the register. But sometimes I take too long and end up late getting back, and then I feel even worse because it’s noticeable. Even when I’m not in front of the mirror, I catch myself comparing my face to girls who come through my line, like checking if their skin looks normal compared to mine. So… I guess I can stop eventually, but it feels like I get pulled into it more than I want to.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're checking your appearance in the mirror because you feel like you have to, or because you want to?\n\nPatient: It’s more like I feel like I have to. I don’t really want to spend my break standing in the bathroom staring at my face, you know? I’ll go in thinking I’m just going to quickly check that spot and then leave, but once I’m there I start fixing my hair around it and looking again, like making sure it’s covered the right way.\n\nAnd then even after I leave the mirror, it doesn’t completely stop, because when I’m back at the register I’ll notice girls coming through my line and compare my face to theirs. Like I’ll think, “Her skin looks normal,” or “She doesn’t have anything weird on her face,” and then I’m right back thinking about mine.\n\nSo I guess it’s not because I enjoy it or because I want to. It feels more like I need to do it so I can get through the shift, but then sometimes I take so long checking or fixing my hair that I’m late getting back to the register, which makes me feel embarrassed.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your checking behavior is aimed at fixing the appearance of the spot on your face, or is it more about following a certain routine or ritual that helps you feel less anxious?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more aimed at fixing it, or at least making it look like it’s not as noticeable. It doesn’t feel like a ritual where I have to do it a certain number of times or in an exact order. It’s more like I see the spot, or I worry it’s showing, and then I need to check if my hair is covering it right.\n\nLike on my breaks, I’ll go into the bathroom and look at my face, and then I’ll move my hair around that side, then look again to see if it helped. And if the lighting makes it look different, I might check from another angle. So there is kind of a pattern, I guess, but it’s not because the pattern itself matters. It’s because I’m trying to make my face look okay enough to go back out to the register.\n\nAnd then when I’m back out there, I end up comparing myself to girls who come through my line, like checking whether their skin looks normal or whether they have anything like mine. That part isn’t fixing it exactly, but it still feels connected to the same thing, like I’m trying to figure out how bad I look compared to them.\n\nSometimes I take too long with all of that and I’m late getting back, which makes me feel really embarrassed. So, yeah, it’s mostly about trying to fix or hide the spot, not doing a routine just for the routine.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you would experience significant distress if you were prevented from checking your appearance in the mirror?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think I would. Maybe not like a full panic or anything, but I’d get really uncomfortable and distracted. Like if I was on my break and I felt like that spot on my face might be showing, and someone told me I couldn’t go check the bathroom mirror, I’d probably keep thinking about it the whole time.\n\nIt’s hard because checking is kind of how I reassure myself enough to go back out to the register. I’ll look at my face, fix my hair around that area, and then look again to see if it seems covered. If I couldn’t do that, I’d be standing there wondering if customers could see it or if it looked worse than I thought.\n\nAnd then when girls come through my line, I’d probably compare my face to theirs even more, like looking at their skin and thinking about how mine looks. So I guess being stopped from checking would make me feel pretty distressed, mostly because I’d feel like I didn’t know whether it looked okay or not. Sometimes I already take too long and end up late getting back, so I know it affects me more than I want it to.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you can stop thinking about the spot on your face if you want to?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, I can try to tell myself to stop, but it doesn’t actually feel like it stops. It’s like once I notice it, especially that tiny uneven spot around my nose and cheek, my brain just keeps going back to it.\n\nThat’s kind of why I came in, because it’s not even something other people always seem to see. Like people will say, “I don’t notice anything,” or “It looks fine,” but then I’ll see it in a photo or under the store lights and it feels so obvious to me. It feels like it’s the first thing customers see when they come up to the register, or like my managers must be noticing it when they talk to me.\n\nEven if I’m trying to focus on work, part of me is still thinking, “Is it showing? Does it look worse in this lighting? Can they tell?” So I wouldn’t say I can just decide not to think about it. I wish I could, because it’s really small, and I know other people say they don’t see it, but to me it feels like it’s right there.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have no control over your need to check your appearance in the mirror?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean… I don’t know if I’d say I have absolutely no control, because technically I can force myself to walk away sometimes. But it feels really hard to stop once I get the thought in my head.\n\nLike on my breaks, if I go into the bathroom and see that spot, I’ll check my face in the mirror, then fix my hair around it, then check again from a slightly different angle. And I’ll tell myself, “Okay, that’s enough, go back,” but then I’ll think, “Wait, what if it moved?” or “What if it looks worse under the front lights?” So I end up staying longer than I meant to.\n\nIt’s embarrassing because I’m supposed to just be taking a quick break, but sometimes I take so long with checking and fixing my hair that I’m late getting back to the register. And even when I do get back, I’ll compare myself to girls who come through my line, like looking at their faces and wondering if mine looks worse.\n\nSo… I guess I have some control, but it doesn’t feel like normal control. It feels like I have to check before I can relax enough to keep working.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're checking your appearance in the mirror because you feel compelled to, or because you actually want to?\n\nPatient: It feels more like I’m compelled to, honestly. I don’t really enjoy it. It’s not like I’m going to the bathroom mirror on my break because I think it’s fun to stare at my face. It’s more like once I start worrying about that spot, I feel like I have to go look, or I won’t be able to settle down.\n\nI’ll check my face in the bathroom mirror, then I’ll move my hair around it or try to cover that area better, and then I’ll look again to make sure it actually helped. And sometimes I keep doing that longer than I mean to, like checking from different angles or seeing if it looks different under the light. I’ll tell myself I’m just going to do it quickly, but then it turns into this whole thing.\n\nAnd then when I go back to the register, I still end up comparing myself to girls who come through my line, like looking at their skin or their face and thinking about how mine must look next to theirs. So I guess it’s not really because I want to. It’s more because I feel like I need to, and if I don’t, I get stuck thinking about it. Sometimes I’ve taken so long fixing my hair and checking that I’m late getting back, which makes me feel embarrassed because I know it’s not normal to spend that much of my break on it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your behavior of checking your appearance is driven more by the desire to fix the perceived flaw or by a feeling that you must check, regardless of the flaw?\n\nPatient: I think it’s kind of both, but if I’m being honest, it starts with wanting to fix it. Like I see or think about that spot on my face, and then I feel like if I just adjust my hair the right way or check it in the bathroom mirror, maybe I can make it less noticeable.\n\nBut then it turns into this feeling that I have to keep checking. Like on my breaks at work, I’ll go to the bathroom and look at my face, fix my hair around that area, then look again, and then maybe look from another angle. It’s not like I’m calmly deciding, “Oh, I’d like to touch up my hair.” It feels more urgent than that, like I can’t really go back to the register until I’ve made sure it looks okay.\n\nAnd even after that, when I’m back at work, I’ll still find myself comparing my face to girls who come through my line, like noticing their skin or how their face looks and then thinking about mine. Sometimes I spend so long checking and fixing my hair that I’m late getting back from my break, which makes me feel really embarrassed.\n\nSo I guess the flaw is what triggers it, but the checking starts feeling like something I have to do, not something I just choose because I want to.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

So, yeah, it’s mostly about trying to fix or hide the spot, not doing a routine just for the routine.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you would experience significant distress if you were prevented from checking your appearance in the mirror?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think I would. Maybe not like a full panic or anything, but I’d get really uncomfortable and distracted. Like if I was on my break and I felt like that spot on my face might be showing, and someone told me I couldn’t go check the bathroom mirror, I’d probably keep thinking about it the whole time.\n\nIt’s hard because checking is kind of how I reassure myself enough to go back out to the register. I’ll look at my face, fix my hair around that area, and then look again to see if it seems covered. If I couldn’t do that, I’d be standing there wondering if customers could see it or if it looked worse than I thought.\n\nAnd then when girls come through my line, I’d probably compare my face to theirs even more, like looking at their skin and thinking about how mine looks. So I guess being stopped from checking would make me feel pretty distressed, mostly because I’d feel like I didn’t know whether it looked okay or not. Sometimes I already take too long and end up late getting back, so I know it affects me more than I want it to.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you can stop thinking about the spot on your face if you want to?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, I can try to tell myself to stop, but it doesn’t actually feel like it stops. It’s like once I notice it, especially that tiny uneven spot around my nose and cheek, my brain just keeps going back to it.\n\nThat’s kind of why I came in, because it’s not even something other people always seem to see. Like people will say, “I don’t notice anything,” or “It looks fine,” but then I’ll see it in a photo or under the store lights and it feels so obvious to me. It feels like it’s the first thing customers see when they come up to the register, or like my managers must be noticing it when they talk to me.\n\nEven if I’m trying to focus on work, part of me is still thinking, “Is it showing? Does it look worse in this lighting? Can they tell?” So I wouldn’t say I can just decide not to think about it. I wish I could, because it’s really small, and I know other people say they don’t see it, but to me it feels like it’s right there.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have no control over your need to check your appearance in the mirror?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean… I don’t know if I’d say I have absolutely no control, because technically I can force myself to walk away sometimes. But it feels really hard to stop once I get the thought in my head.\n\nLike on my breaks, if I go into the bathroom and see that spot, I’ll check my face in the mirror, then fix my hair around it, then check again from a slightly different angle. And I’ll tell myself, “Okay, that’s enough, go back,” but then I’ll think, “Wait, what if it moved?” or “What if it looks worse under the front lights?” So I end up staying longer than I meant to.\n\nIt’s embarrassing because I’m supposed to just be taking a quick break, but sometimes I take so long with checking and fixing my hair that I’m late getting back to the register. And even when I do get back, I’ll compare myself to girls who come through my line, like looking at their faces and wondering if mine looks worse.\n\nSo… I guess I have some control, but it doesn’t feel like normal control. It feels like I have to check before I can relax enough to keep working.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're checking your appearance in the mirror because you feel compelled to, or because you actually want to?\n\nPatient: It feels more like I’m compelled to, honestly. I don’t really enjoy it. It’s not like I’m going to the bathroom mirror on my break because I think it’s fun to stare at my face. It’s more like once I start worrying about that spot, I feel like I have to go look, or I won’t be able to settle down.\n\nI’ll check my face in the bathroom mirror, then I’ll move my hair around it or try to cover that area better, and then I’ll look again to make sure it actually helped. And sometimes I keep doing that longer than I mean to, like checking from different angles or seeing if it looks different under the light. I’ll tell myself I’m just going to do it quickly, but then it turns into this whole thing.\n\nAnd then when I go back to the register, I still end up comparing myself to girls who come through my line, like looking at their skin or their face and thinking about how mine must look next to theirs. So I guess it’s not really because I want to. It’s more because I feel like I need to, and if I don’t, I get stuck thinking about it. Sometimes I’ve taken so long fixing my hair and checking that I’m late getting back, which makes me feel embarrassed because I know it’s not normal to spend that much of my break on it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your behavior of checking your appearance is driven more by the desire to fix the perceived flaw or by a feeling that you must check, regardless of the flaw?\n\nPatient: I think it’s kind of both, but if I’m being honest, it starts with wanting to fix it. Like I see or think about that spot on my face, and then I feel like if I just adjust my hair the right way or check it in the bathroom mirror, maybe I can make it less noticeable.\n\nBut then it turns into this feeling that I have to keep checking. Like on my breaks at work, I’ll go to the bathroom and look at my face, fix my hair around that area, then look again, and then maybe look from another angle. It’s not like I’m calmly deciding, “Oh, I’d like to touch up my hair.” It feels more urgent than that, like I can’t really go back to the register until I’ve made sure it looks okay.\n\nAnd even after that, when I’m back at work, I’ll still find myself comparing my face to girls who come through my line, like noticing their skin or how their face looks and then thinking about mine. Sometimes I spend so long checking and fixing my hair that I’m late getting back from my break, which makes me feel really embarrassed.\n\nSo I guess the flaw is what triggers it, but the checking starts feeling like something I have to do, not something I just choose because I want to.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t think so… or at least, I don’t think I’d check in the same way. Like, if I genuinely didn’t think anything was wrong with my face, I don’t think I’d be spending my breaks in the bathroom mirror like that.

For me it’s usually because I’m worried about that area on my face, and then I start trying to fix my hair around it, like seeing if it covers it better or makes it less obvious. Then I check again, and sometimes I keep looking from different angles, and it just eats up way more time than I meant it to.

If I didn’t think I had a flaw, maybe I’d still glance in the mirror like a normal person, like to make sure my hair isn’t messy or something. But I don’t think I’d get stuck there. And I probably wouldn’t come back to the register still comparing myself to girls who come through my line, looking at their faces and wondering how mine looks next to theirs.

So… no, I think the checking is really tied to thinking something looks wrong. That’s what makes it hard to just leave it alone. Sometimes I take so long checking and fixing my hair that I’m late getting back, and I don’t think that would happen if I wasn’t worried about the flaw.

[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n16\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF45.2, F42\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I guess… I came in because I keep getting really stuck on this tiny uneven spot around my nose and cheek. It’s not even like I can explain it well, but in photos, or when I’m at work under those bright store lights, it just jumps out at me. Like that’s all I can see.\n\nOther people say they don’t notice it, or they’ll say I look fine, but it doesn’t really feel that way to me. It feels like it’s the first thing customers see when they come up to the register, and like my managers must notice it too, even if they don’t say anything. I know it probably sounds small, but it’s been on my mind a lot, and I’m just tired of feeling so uncomfortable about it.\n\nDoctor: Has this concern about your appearance been affecting your work or relationships with others?\n\nPatient: Yeah… mostly at work, I think. I mean, I try to act normal, and I don’t think I’m like completely falling apart or anything, but my manager has noticed I’m coming back from my breaks late. I’ll go to the bathroom or somewhere with a mirror and then I end up taking longer than I meant to, and when I come back I can tell she’s kind of watching the time.\n\nAnd at the register I’ve been moving slower too, like I get distracted and I’m not as quick with customers as I usually am. My manager hasn’t yelled at me or anything, but she has made comments, like reminding me to keep things moving or asking if everything’s okay. And that makes me really nervous because I’m trying to get promoted, and I don’t want them thinking I’m unreliable or not focused enough. That’s probably the biggest thing—feeling like this stupid concern is making me look bad right when I’m trying to prove I can handle more.\n\nDoctor: Is it just this one spot on your face that bothers you, or are there other parts of your appearance that you worry about?\n\nPatient: It’s mostly that one spot, honestly. Like, the area around my nose and cheek where it looks uneven to me. I don’t know if “spot” is even the right word, because sometimes it’s more like the shape or the texture or how the light hits it, especially in photos or under the lights at work.\n\nI guess other things can bother me a little, like anyone, but this is the thing I get stuck on. Even when people tell me they don’t see anything, I still feel like it’s obvious, like customers at the register notice it right away or my managers are looking at it when they talk to me. It’s hard to explain because I know it sounds tiny, but it feels really noticeable to me.\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding social situations or events because of your concern about your appearance?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure how to answer that. I don’t think I’m, like, fully avoiding everything or canceling plans all the time because of it. It’s more that I’m aware of it when I’m around people, if that makes sense.\n\nLike if someone wants to take a picture, I’ll get uncomfortable, or I’ll start thinking about the lighting or which side of my face is showing, and then I’m not really enjoying myself as much. But I don’t know that I’ve stopped going to events completely. I guess I haven’t really thought of it as “avoiding social situations,” exactly. It’s more just… it’s there in the back of my mind when I’m with people.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself repeating certain actions over and over, like checking your appearance in the mirror, in response to your concerns about the spot on your face?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. Probably more than I really want to admit. It’s not like I plan to do it, but on my breaks at work I’ll go into the bathroom and check my face in the mirror, especially that area by my nose and cheek. I’ll look at it from different angles, kind of tilt my head or move closer and farther away, and then I’ll start messing with my hair to try to cover it or make it less noticeable.\n\nAnd then it turns into this whole thing where I’m like, “Okay, just one more second,” but I keep checking again because the lighting looks different or I’m not sure if it looks worse than it did before. Sometimes I’ll compare myself to girls who come through my line too, like noticing their skin or face shape and thinking they look normal and I don’t. I hate that I do that, because they’re just customers and I’m supposed to be working, but my brain goes there.\n\nIt can make my breaks run long because I get stuck in the mirror, and then I’m late getting back to the register. Not by, like, an hour or anything, but enough that it’s been noticed. So yeah, I guess it is repetitive. It feels embarrassing to say, but I do keep checking and fixing and comparing.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like checking your appearance in the mirror helps you feel better or less anxious about the spot on your face?\n\nPatient: For like… a minute, maybe. It’s weird, because I go in there thinking if I just look at it and make sure it’s not as bad as I’m imagining, then I’ll feel calmer and go back to work. And sometimes I do feel a little better if I fix my hair around it or if the mirror lighting makes it look not as noticeable.\n\nBut then it kind of flips. I’ll check from one angle, then another, and then I’m not sure which one is the “real” way people are seeing me. So I keep looking, and if it looks bad in that bathroom mirror, I get more anxious. And even if it looks okay, I don’t totally trust it, because then I think, well, what if it looks different out by the register, or under the store lights, or when customers are standing close?\n\nSo I guess it helps for a second, but not really in a lasting way. I end up fixing my hair around that side of my face and comparing myself to girls who come through my line, and then I’m thinking about it even more. Sometimes I take so long doing all of that on my break that I’m late getting back to the register, which makes me feel embarrassed too.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to check your appearance in the mirror, or is it something you can choose not to do if you want to?\n\nPatient: It feels like I should be able to choose not to, because technically no one is forcing me to go look in the bathroom mirror. But in the moment it doesn’t feel that simple. Like on my break, I’ll tell myself, “Just don’t check, just get your drink or use the bathroom and go back,” but then I start feeling really aware of that spot on my face, and I’ll think maybe my hair moved or maybe it looks worse under the store lights, and then it’s like I can’t really relax until I check.\n\nSo I end up going to the mirror and looking at it, and then I fix my hair around that side of my face, and then I’ll check again from a slightly different angle. It’s embarrassing because it’s not like one quick glance and I’m done. I can get stuck there longer than I meant to, and then I’m late getting back to the register sometimes.\n\nAnd even when I try not to look, I still find myself comparing my face to girls who come through my line, like noticing their skin or their features and thinking they look fine and I don’t. So… I guess I can choose not to in theory, but it feels really hard not to once the thought is there.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're able to stop checking your appearance in the mirror if you want to?\n\nPatient: Not really… I mean, I can sometimes force myself to walk away, but it doesn’t feel easy or like I’m just casually choosing. On breaks at work especially, I’ll tell myself I’m not going to check, because I know if I go into the bathroom mirror it can turn into a whole thing. But then I start wondering if that spot on my face is showing more, or if my hair isn’t covering it right, and I feel like I need to look.\n\nAnd once I do look, it’s hard to stop at one time. I’ll check my face, then fix my hair around that area, then check again to see if it looks better. Sometimes I’ll move around a little because the angle or lighting makes it look different, and then I’m standing there longer than I meant to. I’ll think, “Okay, I’m done,” but then I look one more time before leaving.\n\nIt’s embarrassing because it’s not like I want to spend my break doing that. I’m supposed to just go back to the register. But sometimes I take too long and end up late getting back, and then I feel even worse because it’s noticeable. Even when I’m not in front of the mirror, I catch myself comparing my face to girls who come through my line, like checking if their skin looks normal compared to mine. So… I guess I can stop eventually, but it feels like I get pulled into it more than I want to.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're checking your appearance in the mirror because you feel like you have to, or because you want to?\n\nPatient: It’s more like I feel like I have to. I don’t really want to spend my break standing in the bathroom staring at my face, you know? I’ll go in thinking I’m just going to quickly check that spot and then leave, but once I’m there I start fixing my hair around it and looking again, like making sure it’s covered the right way.\n\nAnd then even after I leave the mirror, it doesn’t completely stop, because when I’m back at the register I’ll notice girls coming through my line and compare my face to theirs. Like I’ll think, “Her skin looks normal,” or “She doesn’t have anything weird on her face,” and then I’m right back thinking about mine.\n\nSo I guess it’s not because I enjoy it or because I want to. It feels more like I need to do it so I can get through the shift, but then sometimes I take so long checking or fixing my hair that I’m late getting back to the register, which makes me feel embarrassed.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your checking behavior is aimed at fixing the appearance of the spot on your face, or is it more about following a certain routine or ritual that helps you feel less anxious?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more aimed at fixing it, or at least making it look like it’s not as noticeable. It doesn’t feel like a ritual where I have to do it a certain number of times or in an exact order. It’s more like I see the spot, or I worry it’s showing, and then I need to check if my hair is covering it right.\n\nLike on my breaks, I’ll go into the bathroom and look at my face, and then I’ll move my hair around that side, then look again to see if it helped. And if the lighting makes it look different, I might check from another angle. So there is kind of a pattern, I guess, but it’s not because the pattern itself matters. It’s because I’m trying to make my face look okay enough to go back out to the register.\n\nAnd then when I’m back out there, I end up comparing myself to girls who come through my line, like checking whether their skin looks normal or whether they have anything like mine. That part isn’t fixing it exactly, but it still feels connected to the same thing, like I’m trying to figure out how bad I look compared to them.\n\nSometimes I take too long with all of that and I’m late getting back, which makes me feel really embarrassed. So, yeah, it’s mostly about trying to fix or hide the spot, not doing a routine just for the routine.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you would experience significant distress if you were prevented from checking your appearance in the mirror?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think I would. Maybe not like a full panic or anything, but I’d get really uncomfortable and distracted. Like if I was on my break and I felt like that spot on my face might be showing, and someone told me I couldn’t go check the bathroom mirror, I’d probably keep thinking about it the whole time.\n\nIt’s hard because checking is kind of how I reassure myself enough to go back out to the register. I’ll look at my face, fix my hair around that area, and then look again to see if it seems covered. If I couldn’t do that, I’d be standing there wondering if customers could see it or if it looked worse than I thought.\n\nAnd then when girls come through my line, I’d probably compare my face to theirs even more, like looking at their skin and thinking about how mine looks. So I guess being stopped from checking would make me feel pretty distressed, mostly because I’d feel like I didn’t know whether it looked okay or not. Sometimes I already take too long and end up late getting back, so I know it affects me more than I want it to.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you can stop thinking about the spot on your face if you want to?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, I can try to tell myself to stop, but it doesn’t actually feel like it stops. It’s like once I notice it, especially that tiny uneven spot around my nose and cheek, my brain just keeps going back to it.\n\nThat’s kind of why I came in, because it’s not even something other people always seem to see. Like people will say, “I don’t notice anything,” or “It looks fine,” but then I’ll see it in a photo or under the store lights and it feels so obvious to me. It feels like it’s the first thing customers see when they come up to the register, or like my managers must be noticing it when they talk to me.\n\nEven if I’m trying to focus on work, part of me is still thinking, “Is it showing? Does it look worse in this lighting? Can they tell?” So I wouldn’t say I can just decide not to think about it. I wish I could, because it’s really small, and I know other people say they don’t see it, but to me it feels like it’s right there.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have no control over your need to check your appearance in the mirror?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean… I don’t know if I’d say I have absolutely no control, because technically I can force myself to walk away sometimes. But it feels really hard to stop once I get the thought in my head.\n\nLike on my breaks, if I go into the bathroom and see that spot, I’ll check my face in the mirror, then fix my hair around it, then check again from a slightly different angle. And I’ll tell myself, “Okay, that’s enough, go back,” but then I’ll think, “Wait, what if it moved?” or “What if it looks worse under the front lights?” So I end up staying longer than I meant to.\n\nIt’s embarrassing because I’m supposed to just be taking a quick break, but sometimes I take so long with checking and fixing my hair that I’m late getting back to the register. And even when I do get back, I’ll compare myself to girls who come through my line, like looking at their faces and wondering if mine looks worse.\n\nSo… I guess I have some control, but it doesn’t feel like normal control. It feels like I have to check before I can relax enough to keep working.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're checking your appearance in the mirror because you feel compelled to, or because you actually want to?\n\nPatient: It feels more like I’m compelled to, honestly. I don’t really enjoy it. It’s not like I’m going to the bathroom mirror on my break because I think it’s fun to stare at my face. It’s more like once I start worrying about that spot, I feel like I have to go look, or I won’t be able to settle down.\n\nI’ll check my face in the bathroom mirror, then I’ll move my hair around it or try to cover that area better, and then I’ll look again to make sure it actually helped. And sometimes I keep doing that longer than I mean to, like checking from different angles or seeing if it looks different under the light. I’ll tell myself I’m just going to do it quickly, but then it turns into this whole thing.\n\nAnd then when I go back to the register, I still end up comparing myself to girls who come through my line, like looking at their skin or their face and thinking about how mine must look next to theirs. So I guess it’s not really because I want to. It’s more because I feel like I need to, and if I don’t, I get stuck thinking about it. Sometimes I’ve taken so long fixing my hair and checking that I’m late getting back, which makes me feel embarrassed because I know it’s not normal to spend that much of my break on it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your behavior of checking your appearance is driven more by the desire to fix the perceived flaw or by a feeling that you must check, regardless of the flaw?\n\nPatient: I think it’s kind of both, but if I’m being honest, it starts with wanting to fix it. Like I see or think about that spot on my face, and then I feel like if I just adjust my hair the right way or check it in the bathroom mirror, maybe I can make it less noticeable.\n\nBut then it turns into this feeling that I have to keep checking. Like on my breaks at work, I’ll go to the bathroom and look at my face, fix my hair around that area, then look again, and then maybe look from another angle. It’s not like I’m calmly deciding, “Oh, I’d like to touch up my hair.” It feels more urgent than that, like I can’t really go back to the register until I’ve made sure it looks okay.\n\nAnd even after that, when I’m back at work, I’ll still find myself comparing my face to girls who come through my line, like noticing their skin or how their face looks and then thinking about mine. Sometimes I spend so long checking and fixing my hair that I’m late getting back from my break, which makes me feel really embarrassed.\n\nSo I guess the flaw is what triggers it, but the checking starts feeling like something I have to do, not something I just choose because I want to.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you would still check your appearance in the mirror even if you didn't think you had any flaws?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… or at least, I don’t think I’d check in the same way. Like, if I genuinely didn’t think anything was wrong with my face, I don’t think I’d be spending my breaks in the bathroom mirror like that.\n\nFor me it’s usually because I’m worried about that area on my face, and then I start trying to fix my hair around it, like seeing if it covers it better or makes it less obvious. Then I check again, and sometimes I keep looking from different angles, and it just eats up way more time than I meant it to.\n\nIf I didn’t think I had a flaw, maybe I’d still glance in the mirror like a normal person, like to make sure my hair isn’t messy or something. But I don’t think I’d get stuck there. And I probably wouldn’t come back to the register still comparing myself to girls who come through my line, looking at their faces and wondering how mine looks next to theirs.\n\nSo… no, I think the checking is really tied to thinking something looks wrong. That’s what makes it hard to just leave it alone. Sometimes I take so long checking and fixing my hair that I’m late getting back, and I don’t think that would happen if I wasn’t worried about the flaw.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I think it starts more with the thought I can’t stop thinking about, like, “Does that part of my face look bad right now?” or “Can people see it?” Once that gets in my head, it’s really hard to just ignore it and keep working like nothing’s happening.

But then it turns into needing to do something about it. Like on my breaks, I’ll go into the bathroom and check my face in the mirror, and then I’ll fix my hair around that area, like trying to make it sit a certain way so it covers it better. Then I look again to see if it helped, and sometimes I keep adjusting it or checking from different angles. It’s not exactly one single action every time, but it feels like I have to check and fix it before I can go back out.

And even when I’m back at the register, I still compare myself to girls who come through my line, like I’ll notice their faces and then think about how mine must look compared to theirs. I don’t really mean to do it, but it happens.

Sometimes I end up taking so long in the bathroom doing all that that I’m late getting back to the register, which makes me feel embarrassed because I know I’m supposed to just be on a quick break. So I guess it’s both, but the thought kind of grabs me first, and then the checking and fixing feels like something I have to do to calm it down.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I guess… I came in because I keep getting really stuck on this tiny uneven spot around my nose and cheek. It’s not even like I can explain it well, but in photos, or when I’m at work under those bright store lights, it just jumps out at me. Like that’s all I can see.\n\nOther people say they don’t notice it, or they’ll say I look fine, but it doesn’t really feel that way to me. It feels like it’s the first thing customers see when they come up to the register, and like my managers must notice it too, even if they don’t say anything. I know it probably sounds small, but it’s been on my mind a lot, and I’m just tired of feeling so uncomfortable about it.\n\nDoctor: Has this concern about your appearance been affecting your work or relationships with others?\n\nPatient: Yeah… mostly at work, I think. I mean, I try to act normal, and I don’t think I’m like completely falling apart or anything, but my manager has noticed I’m coming back from my breaks late. I’ll go to the bathroom or somewhere with a mirror and then I end up taking longer than I meant to, and when I come back I can tell she’s kind of watching the time.\n\nAnd at the register I’ve been moving slower too, like I get distracted and I’m not as quick with customers as I usually am. My manager hasn’t yelled at me or anything, but she has made comments, like reminding me to keep things moving or asking if everything’s okay. And that makes me really nervous because I’m trying to get promoted, and I don’t want them thinking I’m unreliable or not focused enough. That’s probably the biggest thing—feeling like this stupid concern is making me look bad right when I’m trying to prove I can handle more.\n\nDoctor: Is it just this one spot on your face that bothers you, or are there other parts of your appearance that you worry about?\n\nPatient: It’s mostly that one spot, honestly. Like, the area around my nose and cheek where it looks uneven to me. I don’t know if “spot” is even the right word, because sometimes it’s more like the shape or the texture or how the light hits it, especially in photos or under the lights at work.\n\nI guess other things can bother me a little, like anyone, but this is the thing I get stuck on. Even when people tell me they don’t see anything, I still feel like it’s obvious, like customers at the register notice it right away or my managers are looking at it when they talk to me. It’s hard to explain because I know it sounds tiny, but it feels really noticeable to me.\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding social situations or events because of your concern about your appearance?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure how to answer that. I don’t think I’m, like, fully avoiding everything or canceling plans all the time because of it. It’s more that I’m aware of it when I’m around people, if that makes sense.\n\nLike if someone wants to take a picture, I’ll get uncomfortable, or I’ll start thinking about the lighting or which side of my face is showing, and then I’m not really enjoying myself as much. But I don’t know that I’ve stopped going to events completely. I guess I haven’t really thought of it as “avoiding social situations,” exactly. It’s more just… it’s there in the back of my mind when I’m with people.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself repeating certain actions over and over, like checking your appearance in the mirror, in response to your concerns about the spot on your face?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. Probably more than I really want to admit. It’s not like I plan to do it, but on my breaks at work I’ll go into the bathroom and check my face in the mirror, especially that area by my nose and cheek. I’ll look at it from different angles, kind of tilt my head or move closer and farther away, and then I’ll start messing with my hair to try to cover it or make it less noticeable.\n\nAnd then it turns into this whole thing where I’m like, “Okay, just one more second,” but I keep checking again because the lighting looks different or I’m not sure if it looks worse than it did before. Sometimes I’ll compare myself to girls who come through my line too, like noticing their skin or face shape and thinking they look normal and I don’t. I hate that I do that, because they’re just customers and I’m supposed to be working, but my brain goes there.\n\nIt can make my breaks run long because I get stuck in the mirror, and then I’m late getting back to the register. Not by, like, an hour or anything, but enough that it’s been noticed. So yeah, I guess it is repetitive. It feels embarrassing to say, but I do keep checking and fixing and comparing.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like checking your appearance in the mirror helps you feel better or less anxious about the spot on your face?\n\nPatient: For like… a minute, maybe. It’s weird, because I go in there thinking if I just look at it and make sure it’s not as bad as I’m imagining, then I’ll feel calmer and go back to work. And sometimes I do feel a little better if I fix my hair around it or if the mirror lighting makes it look not as noticeable.\n\nBut then it kind of flips. I’ll check from one angle, then another, and then I’m not sure which one is the “real” way people are seeing me. So I keep looking, and if it looks bad in that bathroom mirror, I get more anxious. And even if it looks okay, I don’t totally trust it, because then I think, well, what if it looks different out by the register, or under the store lights, or when customers are standing close?\n\nSo I guess it helps for a second, but not really in a lasting way. I end up fixing my hair around that side of my face and comparing myself to girls who come through my line, and then I’m thinking about it even more. Sometimes I take so long doing all of that on my break that I’m late getting back to the register, which makes me feel embarrassed too.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to check your appearance in the mirror, or is it something you can choose not to do if you want to?\n\nPatient: It feels like I should be able to choose not to, because technically no one is forcing me to go look in the bathroom mirror. But in the moment it doesn’t feel that simple. Like on my break, I’ll tell myself, “Just don’t check, just get your drink or use the bathroom and go back,” but then I start feeling really aware of that spot on my face, and I’ll think maybe my hair moved or maybe it looks worse under the store lights, and then it’s like I can’t really relax until I check.\n\nSo I end up going to the mirror and looking at it, and then I fix my hair around that side of my face, and then I’ll check again from a slightly different angle. It’s embarrassing because it’s not like one quick glance and I’m done. I can get stuck there longer than I meant to, and then I’m late getting back to the register sometimes.\n\nAnd even when I try not to look, I still find myself comparing my face to girls who come through my line, like noticing their skin or their features and thinking they look fine and I don’t. So… I guess I can choose not to in theory, but it feels really hard not to once the thought is there.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're able to stop checking your appearance in the mirror if you want to?\n\nPatient: Not really… I mean, I can sometimes force myself to walk away, but it doesn’t feel easy or like I’m just casually choosing. On breaks at work especially, I’ll tell myself I’m not going to check, because I know if I go into the bathroom mirror it can turn into a whole thing. But then I start wondering if that spot on my face is showing more, or if my hair isn’t covering it right, and I feel like I need to look.\n\nAnd once I do look, it’s hard to stop at one time. I’ll check my face, then fix my hair around that area, then check again to see if it looks better. Sometimes I’ll move around a little because the angle or lighting makes it look different, and then I’m standing there longer than I meant to. I’ll think, “Okay, I’m done,” but then I look one more time before leaving.\n\nIt’s embarrassing because it’s not like I want to spend my break doing that. I’m supposed to just go back to the register. But sometimes I take too long and end up late getting back, and then I feel even worse because it’s noticeable. Even when I’m not in front of the mirror, I catch myself comparing my face to girls who come through my line, like checking if their skin looks normal compared to mine. So… I guess I can stop eventually, but it feels like I get pulled into it more than I want to.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're checking your appearance in the mirror because you feel like you have to, or because you want to?\n\nPatient: It’s more like I feel like I have to. I don’t really want to spend my break standing in the bathroom staring at my face, you know? I’ll go in thinking I’m just going to quickly check that spot and then leave, but once I’m there I start fixing my hair around it and looking again, like making sure it’s covered the right way.\n\nAnd then even after I leave the mirror, it doesn’t completely stop, because when I’m back at the register I’ll notice girls coming through my line and compare my face to theirs. Like I’ll think, “Her skin looks normal,” or “She doesn’t have anything weird on her face,” and then I’m right back thinking about mine.\n\nSo I guess it’s not because I enjoy it or because I want to. It feels more like I need to do it so I can get through the shift, but then sometimes I take so long checking or fixing my hair that I’m late getting back to the register, which makes me feel embarrassed.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your checking behavior is aimed at fixing the appearance of the spot on your face, or is it more about following a certain routine or ritual that helps you feel less anxious?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more aimed at fixing it, or at least making it look like it’s not as noticeable. It doesn’t feel like a ritual where I have to do it a certain number of times or in an exact order. It’s more like I see the spot, or I worry it’s showing, and then I need to check if my hair is covering it right.\n\nLike on my breaks, I’ll go into the bathroom and look at my face, and then I’ll move my hair around that side, then look again to see if it helped. And if the lighting makes it look different, I might check from another angle. So there is kind of a pattern, I guess, but it’s not because the pattern itself matters. It’s because I’m trying to make my face look okay enough to go back out to the register.\n\nAnd then when I’m back out there, I end up comparing myself to girls who come through my line, like checking whether their skin looks normal or whether they have anything like mine. That part isn’t fixing it exactly, but it still feels connected to the same thing, like I’m trying to figure out how bad I look compared to them.\n\nSometimes I take too long with all of that and I’m late getting back, which makes me feel really embarrassed. So, yeah, it’s mostly about trying to fix or hide the spot, not doing a routine just for the routine.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you would experience significant distress if you were prevented from checking your appearance in the mirror?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think I would. Maybe not like a full panic or anything, but I’d get really uncomfortable and distracted. Like if I was on my break and I felt like that spot on my face might be showing, and someone told me I couldn’t go check the bathroom mirror, I’d probably keep thinking about it the whole time.\n\nIt’s hard because checking is kind of how I reassure myself enough to go back out to the register. I’ll look at my face, fix my hair around that area, and then look again to see if it seems covered. If I couldn’t do that, I’d be standing there wondering if customers could see it or if it looked worse than I thought.\n\nAnd then when girls come through my line, I’d probably compare my face to theirs even more, like looking at their skin and thinking about how mine looks. So I guess being stopped from checking would make me feel pretty distressed, mostly because I’d feel like I didn’t know whether it looked okay or not. Sometimes I already take too long and end up late getting back, so I know it affects me more than I want it to.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you can stop thinking about the spot on your face if you want to?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, I can try to tell myself to stop, but it doesn’t actually feel like it stops. It’s like once I notice it, especially that tiny uneven spot around my nose and cheek, my brain just keeps going back to it.\n\nThat’s kind of why I came in, because it’s not even something other people always seem to see. Like people will say, “I don’t notice anything,” or “It looks fine,” but then I’ll see it in a photo or under the store lights and it feels so obvious to me. It feels like it’s the first thing customers see when they come up to the register, or like my managers must be noticing it when they talk to me.\n\nEven if I’m trying to focus on work, part of me is still thinking, “Is it showing? Does it look worse in this lighting? Can they tell?” So I wouldn’t say I can just decide not to think about it. I wish I could, because it’s really small, and I know other people say they don’t see it, but to me it feels like it’s right there.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have no control over your need to check your appearance in the mirror?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean… I don’t know if I’d say I have absolutely no control, because technically I can force myself to walk away sometimes. But it feels really hard to stop once I get the thought in my head.\n\nLike on my breaks, if I go into the bathroom and see that spot, I’ll check my face in the mirror, then fix my hair around it, then check again from a slightly different angle. And I’ll tell myself, “Okay, that’s enough, go back,” but then I’ll think, “Wait, what if it moved?” or “What if it looks worse under the front lights?” So I end up staying longer than I meant to.\n\nIt’s embarrassing because I’m supposed to just be taking a quick break, but sometimes I take so long with checking and fixing my hair that I’m late getting back to the register. And even when I do get back, I’ll compare myself to girls who come through my line, like looking at their faces and wondering if mine looks worse.\n\nSo… I guess I have some control, but it doesn’t feel like normal control. It feels like I have to check before I can relax enough to keep working.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're checking your appearance in the mirror because you feel compelled to, or because you actually want to?\n\nPatient: It feels more like I’m compelled to, honestly. I don’t really enjoy it. It’s not like I’m going to the bathroom mirror on my break because I think it’s fun to stare at my face. It’s more like once I start worrying about that spot, I feel like I have to go look, or I won’t be able to settle down.\n\nI’ll check my face in the bathroom mirror, then I’ll move my hair around it or try to cover that area better, and then I’ll look again to make sure it actually helped. And sometimes I keep doing that longer than I mean to, like checking from different angles or seeing if it looks different under the light. I’ll tell myself I’m just going to do it quickly, but then it turns into this whole thing.\n\nAnd then when I go back to the register, I still end up comparing myself to girls who come through my line, like looking at their skin or their face and thinking about how mine must look next to theirs. So I guess it’s not really because I want to. It’s more because I feel like I need to, and if I don’t, I get stuck thinking about it. Sometimes I’ve taken so long fixing my hair and checking that I’m late getting back, which makes me feel embarrassed because I know it’s not normal to spend that much of my break on it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your behavior of checking your appearance is driven more by the desire to fix the perceived flaw or by a feeling that you must check, regardless of the flaw?\n\nPatient: I think it’s kind of both, but if I’m being honest, it starts with wanting to fix it. Like I see or think about that spot on my face, and then I feel like if I just adjust my hair the right way or check it in the bathroom mirror, maybe I can make it less noticeable.\n\nBut then it turns into this feeling that I have to keep checking. Like on my breaks at work, I’ll go to the bathroom and look at my face, fix my hair around that area, then look again, and then maybe look from another angle. It’s not like I’m calmly deciding, “Oh, I’d like to touch up my hair.” It feels more urgent than that, like I can’t really go back to the register until I’ve made sure it looks okay.\n\nAnd even after that, when I’m back at work, I’ll still find myself comparing my face to girls who come through my line, like noticing their skin or how their face looks and then thinking about mine. Sometimes I spend so long checking and fixing my hair that I’m late getting back from my break, which makes me feel really embarrassed.\n\nSo I guess the flaw is what triggers it, but the checking starts feeling like something I have to do, not something I just choose because I want to.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you would still check your appearance in the mirror even if you didn't think you had any flaws?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… or at least, I don’t think I’d check in the same way. Like, if I genuinely didn’t think anything was wrong with my face, I don’t think I’d be spending my breaks in the bathroom mirror like that.\n\nFor me it’s usually because I’m worried about that area on my face, and then I start trying to fix my hair around it, like seeing if it covers it better or makes it less obvious. Then I check again, and sometimes I keep looking from different angles, and it just eats up way more time than I meant it to.\n\nIf I didn’t think I had a flaw, maybe I’d still glance in the mirror like a normal person, like to make sure my hair isn’t messy or something. But I don’t think I’d get stuck there. And I probably wouldn’t come back to the register still comparing myself to girls who come through my line, looking at their faces and wondering how mine looks next to theirs.\n\nSo… no, I think the checking is really tied to thinking something looks wrong. That’s what makes it hard to just leave it alone. Sometimes I take so long checking and fixing my hair that I’m late getting back, and I don’t think that would happen if I wasn’t worried about the flaw.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're checking your appearance because of a thought that you can't stop thinking about, or because you feel like you have to perform a specific action?\n\nPatient: I think it starts more with the thought I can’t stop thinking about, like, “Does that part of my face look bad right now?” or “Can people see it?” Once that gets in my head, it’s really hard to just ignore it and keep working like nothing’s happening.\n\nBut then it turns into needing to do something about it. Like on my breaks, I’ll go into the bathroom and check my face in the mirror, and then I’ll fix my hair around that area, like trying to make it sit a certain way so it covers it better. Then I look again to see if it helped, and sometimes I keep adjusting it or checking from different angles. It’s not exactly one single action every time, but it feels like I have to check and fix it before I can go back out.\n\nAnd even when I’m back at the register, I still compare myself to girls who come through my line, like I’ll notice their faces and then think about how mine must look compared to theirs. I don’t really mean to do it, but it happens.\n\nSometimes I end up taking so long in the bathroom doing all that that I’m late getting back to the register, which makes me feel embarrassed because I know I’m supposed to just be on a quick break. So I guess it’s both, but the thought kind of grabs me first, and then the checking and fixing feels like something I have to do to calm it down.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."

Sometimes I spend so long checking and fixing my hair that I’m late getting back from my break, which makes me feel really embarrassed.\n\nSo I guess the flaw is what triggers it, but the checking starts feeling like something I have to do, not something I just choose because I want to.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you would still check your appearance in the mirror even if you didn't think you had any flaws?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… or at least, I don’t think I’d check in the same way. Like, if I genuinely didn’t think anything was wrong with my face, I don’t think I’d be spending my breaks in the bathroom mirror like that.\n\nFor me it’s usually because I’m worried about that area on my face, and then I start trying to fix my hair around it, like seeing if it covers it better or makes it less obvious. Then I check again, and sometimes I keep looking from different angles, and it just eats up way more time than I meant it to.\n\nIf I didn’t think I had a flaw, maybe I’d still glance in the mirror like a normal person, like to make sure my hair isn’t messy or something. But I don’t think I’d get stuck there. And I probably wouldn’t come back to the register still comparing myself to girls who come through my line, looking at their faces and wondering how mine looks next to theirs.\n\nSo… no, I think the checking is really tied to thinking something looks wrong. That’s what makes it hard to just leave it alone. Sometimes I take so long checking and fixing my hair that I’m late getting back, and I don’t think that would happen if I wasn’t worried about the flaw.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're checking your appearance because of a thought that you can't stop thinking about, or because you feel like you have to perform a specific action?\n\nPatient: I think it starts more with the thought I can’t stop thinking about, like, “Does that part of my face look bad right now?” or “Can people see it?” Once that gets in my head, it’s really hard to just ignore it and keep working like nothing’s happening.\n\nBut then it turns into needing to do something about it. Like on my breaks, I’ll go into the bathroom and check my face in the mirror, and then I’ll fix my hair around that area, like trying to make it sit a certain way so it covers it better. Then I look again to see if it helped, and sometimes I keep adjusting it or checking from different angles. It’s not exactly one single action every time, but it feels like I have to check and fix it before I can go back out.\n\nAnd even when I’m back at the register, I still compare myself to girls who come through my line, like I’ll notice their faces and then think about how mine must look compared to theirs. I don’t really mean to do it, but it happens.\n\nSometimes I end up taking so long in the bathroom doing all that that I’m late getting back to the register, which makes me feel embarrassed because I know I’m supposed to just be on a quick break. So I guess it’s both, but the thought kind of grabs me first, and then the checking and fixing feels like something I have to do to calm it down.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you feel like you're checking your appearance because you're trying to escape a bad thought, or because you feel like you have to follow a certain rule?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I think it’s more like I’m trying to get away from the bad thought, honestly. Like the thought will pop up that my face looks wrong or that people are noticing it, and then I feel really uncomfortable just standing there at the register pretending I’m fine. So when I get a break, I’ll go to the bathroom mirror and check my face, and then I start fixing my hair around that area to see if I can make it look less obvious.

It’s not really like I have a rule in my head, like “I have to check three times” or do it in a certain exact order. It’s more like I keep checking until I feel a little less panicked about it, but sometimes that takes longer than I expect. I’ll look straight on, then from the side, then move my hair again, and then look again because I’m not sure if it actually helped.

And then even after I go back out, I’ll still catch myself comparing my face to girls who come through my line, like noticing how their skin or face looks and then thinking about mine. I don’t really want to be doing that, but it’s like I’m trying to reassure myself or figure out if I look as bad as I’m afraid I do.

Sometimes I take so long in the bathroom doing the checking and fixing that I’m late getting back to the register, which makes me feel embarrassed. So… yeah, I’d say it’s more about trying to calm down or escape that thought than following some specific rule.

Like on my breaks at work, I’ll go to the bathroom and look at my face, fix my hair around that area, then look again, and then maybe look from another angle. It’s not like I’m calmly deciding, “Oh, I’d like to touch up my hair.” It feels more urgent than that, like I can’t really go back to the register until I’ve made sure it looks okay.\n\nAnd even after that, when I’m back at work, I’ll still find myself comparing my face to girls who come through my line, like noticing their skin or how their face looks and then thinking about mine. Sometimes I spend so long checking and fixing my hair that I’m late getting back from my break, which makes me feel really embarrassed.\n\nSo I guess the flaw is what triggers it, but the checking starts feeling like something I have to do, not something I just choose because I want to.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you would still check your appearance in the mirror even if you didn't think you had any flaws?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… or at least, I don’t think I’d check in the same way. Like, if I genuinely didn’t think anything was wrong with my face, I don’t think I’d be spending my breaks in the bathroom mirror like that.\n\nFor me it’s usually because I’m worried about that area on my face, and then I start trying to fix my hair around it, like seeing if it covers it better or makes it less obvious. Then I check again, and sometimes I keep looking from different angles, and it just eats up way more time than I meant it to.\n\nIf I didn’t think I had a flaw, maybe I’d still glance in the mirror like a normal person, like to make sure my hair isn’t messy or something. But I don’t think I’d get stuck there. And I probably wouldn’t come back to the register still comparing myself to girls who come through my line, looking at their faces and wondering how mine looks next to theirs.\n\nSo… no, I think the checking is really tied to thinking something looks wrong. That’s what makes it hard to just leave it alone. Sometimes I take so long checking and fixing my hair that I’m late getting back, and I don’t think that would happen if I wasn’t worried about the flaw.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're checking your appearance because of a thought that you can't stop thinking about, or because you feel like you have to perform a specific action?\n\nPatient: I think it starts more with the thought I can’t stop thinking about, like, “Does that part of my face look bad right now?” or “Can people see it?” Once that gets in my head, it’s really hard to just ignore it and keep working like nothing’s happening.\n\nBut then it turns into needing to do something about it. Like on my breaks, I’ll go into the bathroom and check my face in the mirror, and then I’ll fix my hair around that area, like trying to make it sit a certain way so it covers it better. Then I look again to see if it helped, and sometimes I keep adjusting it or checking from different angles. It’s not exactly one single action every time, but it feels like I have to check and fix it before I can go back out.\n\nAnd even when I’m back at the register, I still compare myself to girls who come through my line, like I’ll notice their faces and then think about how mine must look compared to theirs. I don’t really mean to do it, but it happens.\n\nSometimes I end up taking so long in the bathroom doing all that that I’m late getting back to the register, which makes me feel embarrassed because I know I’m supposed to just be on a quick break. So I guess it’s both, but the thought kind of grabs me first, and then the checking and fixing feels like something I have to do to calm it down.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're checking your appearance because you're trying to escape a bad thought, or because you feel like you have to follow a certain rule?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more like I’m trying to get away from the bad thought, honestly. Like the thought will pop up that my face looks wrong or that people are noticing it, and then I feel really uncomfortable just standing there at the register pretending I’m fine. So when I get a break, I’ll go to the bathroom mirror and check my face, and then I start fixing my hair around that area to see if I can make it look less obvious.\n\nIt’s not really like I have a rule in my head, like “I have to check three times” or do it in a certain exact order. It’s more like I keep checking until I feel a little less panicked about it, but sometimes that takes longer than I expect. I’ll look straight on, then from the side, then move my hair again, and then look again because I’m not sure if it actually helped.\n\nAnd then even after I go back out, I’ll still catch myself comparing my face to girls who come through my line, like noticing how their skin or face looks and then thinking about mine. I don’t really want to be doing that, but it’s like I’m trying to reassure myself or figure out if I look as bad as I’m afraid I do.\n\nSometimes I take so long in the bathroom doing the checking and fixing that I’m late getting back to the register, which makes me feel embarrassed. So… yeah, I’d say it’s more about trying to calm down or escape that thought than following some specific rule.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

I know it probably sounds small, but it’s been on my mind a lot, and I’m just tired of feeling so uncomfortable about it.\n\nDoctor: Has this concern about your appearance been affecting your work or relationships with others?\n\nPatient: Yeah… mostly at work, I think.
